### [3] SYSTEM-USER prompt · 2026-09-05 10:12:33 UTC

````
<hypothesis>
kind: hypothesis
title: Welfare shields young democracies from inequality
hypothesis: >-
  Among post-1990 democratizers, rising income inequality erodes democratic quality only where welfare institutions are weak:
  the inequality-to-erosion link documented in the global literature is institutionally gated. In countries whose first democratic
  decade built strong universal public services (public health and education systems that the broad middle class actually
  uses), inequality surges do not translate into subsequent declines in the V-Dem Liberal Democracy Index; in countries where
  welfare institutions stayed thin, the same surges predict graded democratic erosion three to five years later. Three mechanisms
  compose the gate: (i) automatic stabilization — universal services absorb market-income shocks before they reach the distribution
  citizens experience; (ii) constituency formation via policy feedback — the educated middle class that uses public services
  holds a material stake in the very institutions that democratic erosion would capture, so it becomes a reservoir of pro-democratic
  resistance; (iii) credible pre-commitment — universal, automatic programs block the 'endogenous limits to redistribution'
  path through which elites respond to redistributive threats by starving redistribution, feeding the populist promise equilibrium
  of Acemoglu, Egorov and Sonin (2013). The gate also explains the co-evolution of inequality, education, and democratic quality:
  the inequality-reduction leg of the democratic dividend (Acemoglu et al., 2015) materialized only where welfare institutions
  were strong; education expanded into absorbed middle-class employment only there; and the size of this first-decade dividend
  predicts resilience two decades later. Corollary: welfare generosity bought later by incumbents (targeted cash, chauvinist
  transfers) does not gate — only the universal service state built before crises does.
motivation: >-
  The field currently holds two contradictory accounts of democratic erosion in the post-1990 wave. The materialist account
  (Houle 2009 for the coup era; Rau and Stokes 2025 for the erosion era) finds inequality is among the strongest predictors
  of democratic decline. The coalitional account (Haggard and Kaufman 2021) argues from close case analysis that economic
  grievances and inequality did not systematically distinguish post-1990 backsliders from survivors, and that polarization
  and elite coalitions did. This contradiction is unresolved because neither side tests the conditioning structure: Rau-Stokes
  estimate a global main effect on uninterrupted democracies with no welfare or education variables and no moderators; Haggard-Kaufman
  use case comparison without an interaction design. This hypothesis resolves the contradiction: inequality matters, but only
  where welfare institutions are too thin to absorb and convert it — both accounts are right in different institutional environments.
  It also extends Acemoglu, Naidu, Restrepo and Robinson's (2015) democratic-dividend result with a mechanism-level explanation
  of its failure: the inequality-reduction leg of the dividend is itself welfare-dependent, which explains why some new democracies
  consolidated and others — despite democratizing — never delivered the dividend and eroded. The finding is directly actionable:
  for post-1990 democratizers and any future democratization wave, the testable policy implication is that universal health
  and education systems must be built in the first democratic decade, before crises arrive. Everything is testable on public
  Our World in Data panels, making the study fully reproducible.
assumptions:
- >-
  The V-Dem Liberal Democracy Index (hosted on OWID) is a valid continuous measure of democratic quality for post-1990 democratizers,
  capturing the graded, executive-aggrandizement form of erosion rather than only discrete regime breakdown.
- >-
  Public health plus public education spending as a share of GDP, measured at the end of the first post-transition decade,
  is a valid pre-determined proxy for universalistic welfare institutions and is not itself determined by subsequent regime
  dynamics (measured before erosion onset).
- >-
  Cross-country comparability of the World Bank PIP Gini coefficient and the World Inequality Database top-10% income share
  is adequate for a country-year panel of post-1990 democratizers.
- >-
  The post-1990 democratization wave (1989-2005 transitions per V-Dem Regimes of the World / Boix-Miller-Rosato) provides
  quasi-exogenous transition timing, with welfare legacies (communist-era health and education systems, colonial-era public
  services) inherited rather than chosen by the new democracies.
- >-
  No unobserved confounder (EU accession, commodity rents, geographic region, initial GDP) drives both welfare-state strength
  and democratic resilience; this is probed by regional fixed effects, within-Eastern-Europe analysis, and placebo specifications.
investigation_approach: >-
  Build an OWID-only country-year panel, 1990-2022, of roughly 35-45 post-1990 democratizers (transitions 1989-2005 by V-Dem
  Regimes of the World), plus all democracies for comparison. Outcome: V-Dem Liberal Democracy Index (level and 5-year differences).
  Inequality treatment: lagged PIP Gini and WID top-10% income share. Welfare measure: WHO domestic general government health
  spending (% GDP) plus World Bank EdStats education spending (% GDP), set to their value at the end of the first democratic
  decade (pre-determined, time-invariant thereafter) — an intentionally non-cash, universal-services measure, contrasted against
  OECD SOCX total social spending (pensions-heavy) as a placebo moderator. Design: (1) two-way fixed-effects regressions with
  country and year fixed effects, interacting inequality with the pre-determined welfare measure — the gate test; (2) adjudication
  specification — baseline inequality effect without interaction (expected weak, matching Haggard-Kaufman) then with interaction
  (expected strong in the thin-welfare arm); (3) erosion-onset event studies with dynamic coefficients estimated separately
  by welfare arm, and lead-lag tests of whether public-services retrenchment and inequality surges precede liberal-democracy
  declines (sequencing vs. reverse causality); (4) democratization event study on inequality and public services by welfare
  legacy — the welfare-dependent dividend test; (5) mechanism arms: V-Dem polarization as mediator (inequality x thin-welfare
  -> polarization -> erosion) and ILO educated-youth-unemployment (education mismatch) as the second gate dimension; (6) robustness:
  alternative inequality measures, alternative welfare measures, population weights, exclusion of commodity booms, within-Eastern-Europe
  estimate, bootstrap confidence intervals for interaction terms. Implemented in Python (linearmodels/statsmodels, clustered
  standard errors), zero external data beyond OWID, no LLM spend.
success_criteria: >-
  CONFIRMATION requires: (a) a significant negative interaction between inequality and welfare strength on liberal-democracy
  trajectories — the within-country inequality effect is near zero (or positive) in the high-welfare arm and strongly negative
  in the thin-welfare arm, robust to all measure and sample swaps; (b) sequencing — inequality surges and public-services
  retrenchment lead liberal-democracy declines by 3-5 years in thin-welfare countries only, with no anticipatory welfare decline
  before erosion onset in the high-welfare arm; (c) the welfare-dependent dividend — democratization reduces the top-10% share
  only in high-welfare-legacy countries, and the size of the first-decade inequality-reduction dividend predicts 2010-2022
  resilience; (d) the mechanism arm — the inequality x thin-welfare interaction raises V-Dem polarization, and education-without-absorption
  (educated-youth unemployment) tightens the gate only where welfare is thin; (e) the universalism contrast — public services
  gate; OECD total social spending does not. DISCONFIRMATION: if the inequality effect is homogeneous across welfare arms
  (no gate), or the sequencing is reversed (erosion precedes welfare decline), or the interaction collapses once GDP level,
  region, and EU accession are controlled, then the hypothesis as stated is falsified — which would itself adjudicate in favor
  of unconditional materialist or purely coalitional accounts.
related_works:
- >-
  Rau and Stokes (2025, PNAS) 'Income inequality and the erosion of democracy in the twenty-first century': the flagship global
  demonstration that inequality predicts democratic erosion (1995-2020, 23 erosion episodes, uninterrupted democracies per
  Miller-Boix-Rosato). It contains no welfare-state or education variables and tests no moderation structure, and its sample
  excludes exactly the post-1990 democratizers that fell below the democracy threshold. This hypothesis adds the institutional
  gate, the young-democracy sample, and a continuous graded outcome.
- >-
  Houle (2009, World Politics) 'Inequality and Democracy: Why Inequality Harms Consolidation but Not Democratization': established
  inequality as a threat to democratic survival, but in a global sample across the coup-driven era with discrete survival
  outcomes and no welfare moderators. The hypothesis extends this to the graded-erosion era and conditions it on welfare institutions.
- >-
  Haggard and Kaufman (2021, 'Backsliding: Democratic Regress in the Contemporary World'): comparative case analysis arguing
  inequality and economic crises did not systematically distinguish post-1990 backsliders, with polarization and elite coalitions
  as proximate causes. The gate hypothesis offers the missing interaction design that can reconcile this with Rau-Stokes:
  inequality matters but only through thin welfare institutions — both accounts hold in different institutional environments.
- >-
  Acemoglu, Naidu, Restrepo and Robinson (2015, AER / NBER w19746) 'Democracy, Redistribution and Inequality': democratization
  raises schooling and public goods and reduces top-decile income shares on average. This hypothesis tests the unexamined
  conditionality of the inequality-reduction leg of that dividend on welfare institutions, and links the dividend's size to
  subsequent regime survival.
- >-
  Acemoglu, Johnson, Robinson and Yared (2005, AER) 'From Education to Democracy?': the load-bearing macro null that education
  does not cause democratization. This hypothesis does not contest the transition margin but moves to the resilience margin,
  where education operates through the welfare-service gate and the labor-absorption (mismatch) channel rather than through
  values socialization alone.
- >-
  Luehrmann (2021, Democratization) 'Disrupting the autocratization sequence': the conceptual framework of autocratization
  as a sequence (rule-of-law weakening, executive aggrandizement, accountability erosion) with institutional resilience as
  the disruptor, but no quantitative welfare-state test. This hypothesis quantifies one specific institution — the universal
  service welfare state — as the gate that disrupts the sequence.
- >-
  Szikra and Oektem (2023, JESP) 'An illiberal welfare state emerging? Welfare efforts and trajectories under democratic backsliding
  in Hungary and Turkey': documents how welfare states are transformed under backsliding (the outcome side). The hypothesis
  tests the opposite direction — whether welfare structure measured before erosion protects — with lead-lag tests that separate
  the two directions empirically.
- >-
  Vanhuysse (2006, 'Divide and Pacify'): strategic social policy as conscious demobilization of protest in early post-communist
  democracies. The hypothesis differs in mechanism (a structural gate on inequality shocks rather than strategic demobilization),
  in scope (the full post-1990 wave, three decades), and in outcome (graded democratic quality rather than protest quiescence),
  and explains the eventual failure of the gate in Hungary and Poland as cases with pension-heavy but service-thin welfare
  states.
- >-
  Campante and Chor (2012, QJE) 'Why Was the Arab World Poised for Revolution?': schooling interacted with poor economic opportunities
  predicts protest onset. The hypothesis transposes the mismatch mechanism from protest onset to graded democratic erosion
  across all post-1990 democratizers, and adds the welfare-state gate that Campante-Chor do not consider.
inspiration: >-
  Three cross-domain transfers structure the hypothesis. (1) Control theory — disturbance rejection: engineering asks what
  determines the gain with which an exogenous disturbance propagates to a state variable one cares about. Here the disturbance
  is the inequality shock, the state variable is democratic quality, and the welfare state is the feedback controller whose
  gain determines transmission; the empirical translation of 'disturbance rejection' is exactly the interaction (gate) design
  that this literature has not run. (2) Public-health effect modification: epidemiology does not ask only whether a risk factor
  harms, but whether modifiable protective exposures change the effect of exposure (clean water changes the effect of pathogens).
  The same logic — institutions as modifiable protective exposures that gate a socioeconomic risk factor's population-level
  harm — is standard in epidemiology but almost absent from the inequality-democracy literature, which estimates main effects.
  (3) Ecology's resistance-versus-resilience distinction: the fate of a disturbed system depends on its buffering capacity,
  not the disturbance alone; universal welfare institutions are the system's buffering capacity. The mechanism inside the
  gate is built from the reviewer's own theoretical toolkit — Acemoglu, Egorov and Sonin's (2013) populism equilibrium (elites
  endogenously limit redistribution, making populists' redistributive promises decisive) and Acemoglu-Robinson's democratization-as-commitment
  logic — combined with Esping-Andersen's decommodification and the policy-feedback insight that programs create their own
  defenders.
terms:
- term: Liberal Democracy Index
  definition: >-
    V-Dem's v2x_libdem index (0-1), combining electoral democracy with constraints on the executive and protection of civil
    liberties; hosted on Our World in Data; the outcome measure of democratic quality in this hypothesis.
- term: Post-1990 democratizers
  definition: >-
    Countries whose transition to electoral democracy began between 1989 and 2005, coded via V-Dem Regimes of the World and
    the Boix-Miller-Rosato regime classification; the treated sample of young democracies.
- term: Democratic erosion (backsliding)
  definition: >-
    Gradual, incremental decline in democratic quality — executive aggrandizement, weakened accountability, curtailed civil
    liberties — within a formally democratic regime, as opposed to sudden breakdown by coup.
- term: Welfare institutions
  definition: >-
    The universalistic service state: public health and public education spending as a share of GDP, which benefit broad segments
    of the population across the income distribution, as opposed to targeted cash transfers or pension-heavy social spending.
- term: Institutional gate (moderation)
  definition: >-
    The claim that the effect of one variable (inequality) on another (democratic quality) is conditional on a third (welfare
    institutions); the empirical expression is an interaction term, distinguishing this design from mediation-style 'through
    which channel' claims.
- term: Democratic dividend
  definition: >-
    The improvements in growth, schooling, public goods, and income distribution observed after democratization (Acemoglu
    et al. 2015); this hypothesis isolates the inequality-reduction leg and tests its dependence on welfare institutions.
- term: Decommodification
  definition: >-
    Esping-Andersen's concept: the degree to which welfare institutions sever living standards from labor-market position,
    so that market shocks are not immediately transmitted into citizens' welfare and political grievances.
- term: Policy feedback
  definition: >-
    The mechanism by which existing policies reshape politics — programs create constituencies with a material stake in preserving
    them, converting welfare beneficiaries into defenders of the public institutions democratic erosion would capture.
- term: Endogenous limits to redistribution
  definition: >-
    The Acemoglu-Egorov-Sonin (2013) result that high inequality induces elites to reduce redistribution strategically, so
    the median voter's demands go unmet and a populist promising redistribution can win — an equilibrium that universal automatic
    welfare programs pre-empt by committing redistribution in advance.
- term: Educated-youth unemployment
  definition: >-
    Unemployment among young people (15-24) with completed education, from the ILO Education and Mismatch Indicators on OWID;
    the operational measure of educational expansion without labor-market absorption.
summary: >-
  Rising inequality predicts democratic erosion among post-1990 democratizers only where welfare institutions are thin: universal
  public health and education systems built in the first democratic decade gate the inequality-erosion link, and the inequality-reduction
  'democratic dividend' of democracy itself materializes only behind that gate. Testable entirely on OWID panels, this welfare-gate
  account reconciles the global inequality-erosion finding (Rau-Stokes 2025) with the case-based skepticism about economic
  drivers (Haggard-Kaufman 2021) and explains which post-1990 democratizers consolidated and which backslid.
</hypothesis>

id: research_iter1_dir3
type: research
objective: >-
  Extract exact replication and theory specifications from the prior literature so iteration 2's experiment adjudicates Rau-Stokes
  vs Haggard-Kaufman through the welfare gate rather than reinventing either: Rau & Stokes (2025) - sample and erosion-episode
  list, outcome coding, controls, model type, clustering, and period; Acemoglu, Naidu, Restrepo & Robinson (2015) - the democratization
  dividend design (outcomes: top-decile share, public goods, schooling; sample; event-study timing) whose inequality-reduction
  leg the hypothesis claims is welfare-gated; Houle (2009) - survival-model setup and inequality operationalization; Campante
  & Chor (2012) - the schooling-times-opportunity interaction design as the template for the education-mismatch arm; Haggard
  & Kaufman (2021) - the backslider/survivor case list and the evidence behind their claim that grievances did not differentiate
  outcomes; Acemoglu, Egorov & Sonin (2013) - the endogenous-limits-to-redistribution logic to operationalize as the pre-commitment
  mechanism; plus micro-evidence on policy feedback and universalism to justify the constituency-formation mechanism.
approach: >-
  Web research in scholarly mode with PDF mining: locate each paper (PNAS/arXiv/journal), fetch the text, and use fetch_grep
  to capture exact model equations, sample definitions, variable operationalizations, controls, and case lists. Synthesize
  into a design-briefing report that maps each hypothesis mechanism to a testable specification - the gate interaction, the
  adjudication specification (baseline main effect without interaction, then with), erosion-onset event studies with lead-lag
  sequencing, the welfare-dependent dividend test, and the polarization/mismatch mechanism arms - and flag the divergences
  the gate design must bridge (notably Rau-Stokes's uninterrupted-democracy sample vs young democratizers, and SOCX's pension-heavy
  composition vs the universal-services measure).

### [16] SYSTEM-USER prompt · 2026-09-05 10:24:33 UTC

````
<hypothesis>
kind: hypothesis
title: Welfare shields young democracies from inequality
hypothesis: >-
  Among post-1990 democratizers, rising income inequality erodes democratic quality only where welfare institutions are weak:
  the inequality-to-erosion link documented in the global literature is institutionally gated. In countries whose first democratic
  decade built strong universal public services (public health and education systems that the broad middle class actually
  uses), inequality surges do not translate into subsequent declines in the V-Dem Liberal Democracy Index; in countries where
  welfare institutions stayed thin, the same surges predict graded democratic erosion three to five years later. Three mechanisms
  compose the gate: (i) automatic stabilization — universal services absorb market-income shocks before they reach the distribution
  citizens experience; (ii) constituency formation via policy feedback — the educated middle class that uses public services
  holds a material stake in the very institutions that democratic erosion would capture, so it becomes a reservoir of pro-democratic
  resistance; (iii) credible pre-commitment — universal, automatic programs block the 'endogenous limits to redistribution'
  path through which elites respond to redistributive threats by starving redistribution, feeding the populist promise equilibrium
  of Acemoglu, Egorov and Sonin (2013). The gate also explains the co-evolution of inequality, education, and democratic quality:
  the inequality-reduction leg of the democratic dividend (Acemoglu et al., 2015) materialized only where welfare institutions
  were strong; education expanded into absorbed middle-class employment only there; and the size of this first-decade dividend
  predicts resilience two decades later. Corollary: welfare generosity bought later by incumbents (targeted cash, chauvinist
  transfers) does not gate — only the universal service state built before crises does.
motivation: >-
  The field currently holds two contradictory accounts of democratic erosion in the post-1990 wave. The materialist account
  (Houle 2009 for the coup era; Rau and Stokes 2025 for the erosion era) finds inequality is among the strongest predictors
  of democratic decline. The coalitional account (Haggard and Kaufman 2021) argues from close case analysis that economic
  grievances and inequality did not systematically distinguish post-1990 backsliders from survivors, and that polarization
  and elite coalitions did. This contradiction is unresolved because neither side tests the conditioning structure: Rau-Stokes
  estimate a global main effect on uninterrupted democracies with no welfare or education variables and no moderators; Haggard-Kaufman
  use case comparison without an interaction design. This hypothesis resolves the contradiction: inequality matters, but only
  where welfare institutions are too thin to absorb and convert it — both accounts are right in different institutional environments.
  It also extends Acemoglu, Naidu, Restrepo and Robinson's (2015) democratic-dividend result with a mechanism-level explanation
  of its failure: the inequality-reduction leg of the dividend is itself welfare-dependent, which explains why some new democracies
  consolidated and others — despite democratizing — never delivered the dividend and eroded. The finding is directly actionable:
  for post-1990 democratizers and any future democratization wave, the testable policy implication is that universal health
  and education systems must be built in the first democratic decade, before crises arrive. Everything is testable on public
  Our World in Data panels, making the study fully reproducible.
assumptions:
- >-
  The V-Dem Liberal Democracy Index (hosted on OWID) is a valid continuous measure of democratic quality for post-1990 democratizers,
  capturing the graded, executive-aggrandizement form of erosion rather than only discrete regime breakdown.
- >-
  Public health plus public education spending as a share of GDP, measured at the end of the first post-transition decade,
  is a valid pre-determined proxy for universalistic welfare institutions and is not itself determined by subsequent regime
  dynamics (measured before erosion onset).
- >-
  Cross-country comparability of the World Bank PIP Gini coefficient and the World Inequality Database top-10% income share
  is adequate for a country-year panel of post-1990 democratizers.
- >-
  The post-1990 democratization wave (1989-2005 transitions per V-Dem Regimes of the World / Boix-Miller-Rosato) provides
  quasi-exogenous transition timing, with welfare legacies (communist-era health and education systems, colonial-era public
  services) inherited rather than chosen by the new democracies.
- >-
  No unobserved confounder (EU accession, commodity rents, geographic region, initial GDP) drives both welfare-state strength
  and democratic resilience; this is probed by regional fixed effects, within-Eastern-Europe analysis, and placebo specifications.
investigation_approach: >-
  Build an OWID-only country-year panel, 1990-2022, of roughly 35-45 post-1990 democratizers (transitions 1989-2005 by V-Dem
  Regimes of the World), plus all democracies for comparison. Outcome: V-Dem Liberal Democracy Index (level and 5-year differences).
  Inequality treatment: lagged PIP Gini and WID top-10% income share. Welfare measure: WHO domestic general government health
  spending (% GDP) plus World Bank EdStats education spending (% GDP), set to their value at the end of the first democratic
  decade (pre-determined, time-invariant thereafter) — an intentionally non-cash, universal-services measure, contrasted against
  OECD SOCX total social spending (pensions-heavy) as a placebo moderator. Design: (1) two-way fixed-effects regressions with
  country and year fixed effects, interacting inequality with the pre-determined welfare measure — the gate test; (2) adjudication
  specification — baseline inequality effect without interaction (expected weak, matching Haggard-Kaufman) then with interaction
  (expected strong in the thin-welfare arm); (3) erosion-onset event studies with dynamic coefficients estimated separately
  by welfare arm, and lead-lag tests of whether public-services retrenchment and inequality surges precede liberal-democracy
  declines (sequencing vs. reverse causality); (4) democratization event study on inequality and public services by welfare
  legacy — the welfare-dependent dividend test; (5) mechanism arms: V-Dem polarization as mediator (inequality x thin-welfare
  -> polarization -> erosion) and ILO educated-youth-unemployment (education mismatch) as the second gate dimension; (6) robustness:
  alternative inequality measures, alternative welfare measures, population weights, exclusion of commodity booms, within-Eastern-Europe
  estimate, bootstrap confidence intervals for interaction terms. Implemented in Python (linearmodels/statsmodels, clustered
  standard errors), zero external data beyond OWID, no LLM spend.
success_criteria: >-
  CONFIRMATION requires: (a) a significant negative interaction between inequality and welfare strength on liberal-democracy
  trajectories — the within-country inequality effect is near zero (or positive) in the high-welfare arm and strongly negative
  in the thin-welfare arm, robust to all measure and sample swaps; (b) sequencing — inequality surges and public-services
  retrenchment lead liberal-democracy declines by 3-5 years in thin-welfare countries only, with no anticipatory welfare decline
  before erosion onset in the high-welfare arm; (c) the welfare-dependent dividend — democratization reduces the top-10% share
  only in high-welfare-legacy countries, and the size of the first-decade inequality-reduction dividend predicts 2010-2022
  resilience; (d) the mechanism arm — the inequality x thin-welfare interaction raises V-Dem polarization, and education-without-absorption
  (educated-youth unemployment) tightens the gate only where welfare is thin; (e) the universalism contrast — public services
  gate; OECD total social spending does not. DISCONFIRMATION: if the inequality effect is homogeneous across welfare arms
  (no gate), or the sequencing is reversed (erosion precedes welfare decline), or the interaction collapses once GDP level,
  region, and EU accession are controlled, then the hypothesis as stated is falsified — which would itself adjudicate in favor
  of unconditional materialist or purely coalitional accounts.
related_works:
- >-
  Rau and Stokes (2025, PNAS) 'Income inequality and the erosion of democracy in the twenty-first century': the flagship global
  demonstration that inequality predicts democratic erosion (1995-2020, 23 erosion episodes, uninterrupted democracies per
  Miller-Boix-Rosato). It contains no welfare-state or education variables and tests no moderation structure, and its sample
  excludes exactly the post-1990 democratizers that fell below the democracy threshold. This hypothesis adds the institutional
  gate, the young-democracy sample, and a continuous graded outcome.
- >-
  Houle (2009, World Politics) 'Inequality and Democracy: Why Inequality Harms Consolidation but Not Democratization': established
  inequality as a threat to democratic survival, but in a global sample across the coup-driven era with discrete survival
  outcomes and no welfare moderators. The hypothesis extends this to the graded-erosion era and conditions it on welfare institutions.
- >-
  Haggard and Kaufman (2021, 'Backsliding: Democratic Regress in the Contemporary World'): comparative case analysis arguing
  inequality and economic crises did not systematically distinguish post-1990 backsliders, with polarization and elite coalitions
  as proximate causes. The gate hypothesis offers the missing interaction design that can reconcile this with Rau-Stokes:
  inequality matters but only through thin welfare institutions — both accounts hold in different institutional environments.
- >-
  Acemoglu, Naidu, Restrepo and Robinson (2015, AER / NBER w19746) 'Democracy, Redistribution and Inequality': democratization
  raises schooling and public goods and reduces top-decile income shares on average. This hypothesis tests the unexamined
  conditionality of the inequality-reduction leg of that dividend on welfare institutions, and links the dividend's size to
  subsequent regime survival.
- >-
  Acemoglu, Johnson, Robinson and Yared (2005, AER) 'From Education to Democracy?': the load-bearing macro null that education
  does not cause democratization. This hypothesis does not contest the transition margin but moves to the resilience margin,
  where education operates through the welfare-service gate and the labor-absorption (mismatch) channel rather than through
  values socialization alone.
- >-
  Luehrmann (2021, Democratization) 'Disrupting the autocratization sequence': the conceptual framework of autocratization
  as a sequence (rule-of-law weakening, executive aggrandizement, accountability erosion) with institutional resilience as
  the disruptor, but no quantitative welfare-state test. This hypothesis quantifies one specific institution — the universal
  service welfare state — as the gate that disrupts the sequence.
- >-
  Szikra and Oektem (2023, JESP) 'An illiberal welfare state emerging? Welfare efforts and trajectories under democratic backsliding
  in Hungary and Turkey': documents how welfare states are transformed under backsliding (the outcome side). The hypothesis
  tests the opposite direction — whether welfare structure measured before erosion protects — with lead-lag tests that separate
  the two directions empirically.
- >-
  Vanhuysse (2006, 'Divide and Pacify'): strategic social policy as conscious demobilization of protest in early post-communist
  democracies. The hypothesis differs in mechanism (a structural gate on inequality shocks rather than strategic demobilization),
  in scope (the full post-1990 wave, three decades), and in outcome (graded democratic quality rather than protest quiescence),
  and explains the eventual failure of the gate in Hungary and Poland as cases with pension-heavy but service-thin welfare
  states.
- >-
  Campante and Chor (2012, QJE) 'Why Was the Arab World Poised for Revolution?': schooling interacted with poor economic opportunities
  predicts protest onset. The hypothesis transposes the mismatch mechanism from protest onset to graded democratic erosion
  across all post-1990 democratizers, and adds the welfare-state gate that Campante-Chor do not consider.
inspiration: >-
  Three cross-domain transfers structure the hypothesis. (1) Control theory — disturbance rejection: engineering asks what
  determines the gain with which an exogenous disturbance propagates to a state variable one cares about. Here the disturbance
  is the inequality shock, the state variable is democratic quality, and the welfare state is the feedback controller whose
  gain determines transmission; the empirical translation of 'disturbance rejection' is exactly the interaction (gate) design
  that this literature has not run. (2) Public-health effect modification: epidemiology does not ask only whether a risk factor
  harms, but whether modifiable protective exposures change the effect of exposure (clean water changes the effect of pathogens).
  The same logic — institutions as modifiable protective exposures that gate a socioeconomic risk factor's population-level
  harm — is standard in epidemiology but almost absent from the inequality-democracy literature, which estimates main effects.
  (3) Ecology's resistance-versus-resilience distinction: the fate of a disturbed system depends on its buffering capacity,
  not the disturbance alone; universal welfare institutions are the system's buffering capacity. The mechanism inside the
  gate is built from the reviewer's own theoretical toolkit — Acemoglu, Egorov and Sonin's (2013) populism equilibrium (elites
  endogenously limit redistribution, making populists' redistributive promises decisive) and Acemoglu-Robinson's democratization-as-commitment
  logic — combined with Esping-Andersen's decommodification and the policy-feedback insight that programs create their own
  defenders.
terms:
- term: Liberal Democracy Index
  definition: >-
    V-Dem's v2x_libdem index (0-1), combining electoral democracy with constraints on the executive and protection of civil
    liberties; hosted on Our World in Data; the outcome measure of democratic quality in this hypothesis.
- term: Post-1990 democratizers
  definition: >-
    Countries whose transition to electoral democracy began between 1989 and 2005, coded via V-Dem Regimes of the World and
    the Boix-Miller-Rosato regime classification; the treated sample of young democracies.
- term: Democratic erosion (backsliding)
  definition: >-
    Gradual, incremental decline in democratic quality — executive aggrandizement, weakened accountability, curtailed civil
    liberties — within a formally democratic regime, as opposed to sudden breakdown by coup.
- term: Welfare institutions
  definition: >-
    The universalistic service state: public health and public education spending as a share of GDP, which benefit broad segments
    of the population across the income distribution, as opposed to targeted cash transfers or pension-heavy social spending.
- term: Institutional gate (moderation)
  definition: >-
    The claim that the effect of one variable (inequality) on another (democratic quality) is conditional on a third (welfare
    institutions); the empirical expression is an interaction term, distinguishing this design from mediation-style 'through
    which channel' claims.
- term: Democratic dividend
  definition: >-
    The improvements in growth, schooling, public goods, and income distribution observed after democratization (Acemoglu
    et al. 2015); this hypothesis isolates the inequality-reduction leg and tests its dependence on welfare institutions.
- term: Decommodification
  definition: >-
    Esping-Andersen's concept: the degree to which welfare institutions sever living standards from labor-market position,
    so that market shocks are not immediately transmitted into citizens' welfare and political grievances.
- term: Policy feedback
  definition: >-
    The mechanism by which existing policies reshape politics — programs create constituencies with a material stake in preserving
    them, converting welfare beneficiaries into defenders of the public institutions democratic erosion would capture.
- term: Endogenous limits to redistribution
  definition: >-
    The Acemoglu-Egorov-Sonin (2013) result that high inequality induces elites to reduce redistribution strategically, so
    the median voter's demands go unmet and a populist promising redistribution can win — an equilibrium that universal automatic
    welfare programs pre-empt by committing redistribution in advance.
- term: Educated-youth unemployment
  definition: >-
    Unemployment among young people (15-24) with completed education, from the ILO Education and Mismatch Indicators on OWID;
    the operational measure of educational expansion without labor-market absorption.
summary: >-
  Rising inequality predicts democratic erosion among post-1990 democratizers only where welfare institutions are thin: universal
  public health and education systems built in the first democratic decade gate the inequality-erosion link, and the inequality-reduction
  'democratic dividend' of democracy itself materializes only behind that gate. Testable entirely on OWID panels, this welfare-gate
  account reconciles the global inequality-erosion finding (Rau-Stokes 2025) with the case-based skepticism about economic
  drivers (Haggard-Kaufman 2021) and explains which post-1990 democratizers consolidated and which backslid.
</hypothesis>

objective: >-

  vs Haggard-Kaufman through the welfare gate rather than reinventing either: Rau & Stokes (2025) - sample and erosion-episode
  list, outcome coding, controls, model type, clustering, and period; Acemoglu, Naidu, Restrepo & Robinson (2015) - the democratization
  dividend design (outcomes: top-decile share, public goods, schooling; sample; event-study timing) whose inequality-reduction

  mechanism; plus micro-evidence on policy feedback and universalism to justify the constituency-formation mechanism.

  composition vs the universal-services measure).

---
STEP-BY-STEP RESEARCH PLAN (3 h budget; zero LLM spend — pure web research)

ROLE & DELIVERABLES
You are the RESEARCH executor. Produce exactly:
1. research_report.md — the "design briefing" (main deliverable; see §10 for structure)
2. research_out.json — {"answer": <concise synthesis>, "sources": [{title,url,doi,contribution}...], "follow_up_questions": [...]}
All files inside this workspace. Save per-paper extraction notes under notes/ as you go.

TOOLING (read aii-web-tools skill first)
- Use built-in WebSearch/WebFetch if available (ToolSearch select:WebSearch,WebFetch once). Else use the skill's scripts: aii_fast_web_search.py --query ... --mode scholarly (OpenAlex/Crossref for academic metadata; mode general for discovery) and aii_fast_web_fetch.py fetch|grep.
- Workflow per source: search (scholarly) → fetch (gist) → grep (exact numbers, equations, case lists). Grep PDFs with context windows; on HTML try fetch first.
- Parallelize aggressively: launch 4-6 independent searches/fetches per turn; only sequence fetch-after-search. Save outputs to notes/<name>.txt as you go so nothing is lost on a timeout.
- VERIFICATION RULE: every number, case, and equation you record must be traced to a fetched source (URL + quote snippet in your notes). Never guess citations or DOIs: Crossref/OpenAlex search first. If a source is paywalled, mark it PAYWALLED-ABSTRACT-ONLY and record what the abstract/Google Books/review does give; do NOT invent details; route missing items to follow_up_questions.

PHASE 0 — DISCOVERY & SOURCE LOCK (0:00-0:15)
Run one scholarly search per target in parallel, with mode=scholarly:
1. "Rau Stokes income inequality erosion of democracy PNAS 2025"
2. "Acemoglu Naidu Restrepo Robinson democracy redistribution inequality"
3. "Houle inequality and democracy why inequality harms consolidation"
4. "Campante Chor why was the Arab world poised for revolution"
5. "Acemoglu Egorov Sonin political theory of populism"
6. "Haggard Kaufman backsliding democratic regress contemporary world"
7. "Korpi Palme paradox of redistribution strategies of equality"
8. "Pierson when effect becomes cause policy feedback"
Lock: exact authors, year, journal, volume/pages, DOI, and a URL you can fetch (PNAS page + SI PDF; NBER working-paper PDFs; author-hosted PDFs; ScienceDirect/Sage/OUP landing pages; Google Books for the CUP book). Write to notes/00_sources.md with a status column (FOUND/FETCHED/EXTRACTED/PAYWALLED).
Also confirm the two dataset papers: Lührmann-Tannenberg-Lindberg "Regimes of the World" (Democratization 2018) and Boix-Miller-Rosato "A Complete Data Set of Political Regimes 1800-2007" (CPS 2013) — these define the transition dates and the "uninterrupted democracy" sample the experiment inherits.

PHASE 1 — EXTRACTION DOSSIERS (0:15-2:15, ~120 min)
For each workstream, do: fetch main text → grep exact items → record into notes/<name>.md. (Priorities: A, B, E, D are load-bearing; trim G/H first under time pressure.)

A. Rau & Stokes (2025, PNAS) — the flagship main effect [25 min]
Locate: PNAS DOI (search "pnas site: pnas.org income inequality erosion democracy") and the supplementary PDF (pnas.*.sapp.pdf) holding full tables. grep patterns: "interrupted", "sample", "erosion", "episode", "Gini|SWIID|PIP", "hazard|Cox|logit|OLS", "control", "cluster", "lag", "1995|2000|2020", "threshold".
Record: (a) exact sample definition — which regime classification, which countries excluded (post-1990 democratizers that fell below threshold?), N countries, N country-years, period; (b) the erosion-episode list and coding rule (binary onset? sustained LDI drop? which V-Dem index?); (c) inequality variable: exact source (SWIID/PIP/WID), transformation, lag; (d) full control set; (e) model family + clustering + fixed effects; (f) headline estimates with SEs (for magnitude calibration of the iter-2 main effect); (g) any moderation analyses they did or explicitly did not do; (h) replication-data availability mention. Also search for published commentary/rejoinder or replication critique within 5 min.

B. Acemoglu, Naidu, Restrepo & Robinson (2015) — the dividend design [30 min]
Locate: NBER w19746 (free PDF: nber.org/papers/w19746) + the published version (VERIFY venue: the hypothesis says AER; expected reality: chapter in Handbook of Income Distribution Vol 2, Elsevier, 2015 — confirm via Crossref/OpenAlex and cite the real venue). grep patterns: "democratization", "event study", "top decile|top 10", "schooling|education", "tax", "public good", "matching", "1960", "equation", "cluster", "sample".
Record: (a) democratization-event definition & classification source; (b) sample: N countries, years; (c) full outcome list: top-decile/10% income share (WID?), tax share, social/education/health spending, schooling; (d) estimation: OLS with country-year FE? dynamic leads/lags event-study form? matching? report their actual equation(s) as stated; (e) controls and clustering; (f) headline magnitudes: e.g., effect of democratization on top-decile share at +10y, on public-goods measures — needed to power the iter-2 "welfare-dependent dividend" test; (g) their own caveats on the inequality leg (which is exactly the leg the hypothesis claims is welfare-gated). Skim ANRR 2019 JPE "Democracy Does Cause Growth" only for event-study window/timing conventions (10 min max).

C. Houle (2009, World Politics) — survival/consolidation setup [15 min]
Locate: Cambridge Core landing page (abstract) + a full-text PDF (search "Houle inequality democracy world politics 2009 pdf" — his MSU faculty page hosts it; Project MUSE as fallback for abstract+first pages). grep: "hazard|survival|Weibull|Cox", "Gini", "lag|five-year|moving average", "control", "sample", "consolidation", "breakdown".
Record: (a) outcome & sample (democracies, years, N breakdowns); (b) inequality operationalization (source WIID/Deininger-Squire? interpolation? lagged moving average?); (c) model family (discrete-time? Cox? duration dependency?), controls, clustering; (d) key coefficients & interpretation for the young-democracy consolidation frame; (e) his definition of consolidation as survival (used to justify "first democratic decade" framing).

D. Campante & Chor (2012, QJE) — the mismatch-interaction template [20 min]
Locate: NBER w16752 PDF (free) or Chor's faculty page PDF; QJE page for final version. grep: "interaction", "schooling", "opportunit", "unemployment", "instrument", "probit|logit", "Arab", "Figure|Table", "coefficient".
Record: (a) unit of analysis (country-level and individual Arab Barometer); (b) exact DV (protest onset/intensity coding); (c) the two interacted regressors and their construction — average/expansion of schooling on one hand, the "economic opportunities" measure on the other (which components: educated-youth unemployment? income? corruption?); (d) the interaction equation as printed and the sign pattern (schooling raises protest only where opportunities are poor); (e) endogeneity treatment (IV? lagged schooling growth?); (f) headline marginal effects for calibrating the iter-2 mismatch arm (ILO educated-youth unemployment on OWID).

E. Haggard & Kaufman (2021) — the coalitional null + case list [25 min]
Locate: CUP book page; Google Books preview (search inside for "backslid" introduction); their Journal of Democracy pieces ("Backsliding: Democratic Regress in the Contemporary World", JD 2021; verify existence); interviews/reviews (Perspectives on Politics, APSR review) that summarize the case list; their 2016 Annual Review of Political Science "Democratization During the Third Wave" for welfare-state development in new democracies. grep: "backslider", "case", "Hungary", "Poland", "Turkey", "Venezuela", "South Africa", "inequality", "grievance", "polariz", "did not|no evidence".
Record: (a) their operational definition of backsliding (index, threshold, period); (b) the complete backslider list (and survivors they compare against) — critical for iter-2's sensitivity analysis ("does the gate hold within H&K's own classification?"); (c) the exact evidence for "grievances/inequality did not differentiate": any comparison of inequality trends/crises between backsliders and survivors (tables/graphs), and their polarization measures (V-Dem v2x_polarization? their own coding?); (d) their welfare-state material if any (2008 book, 2016 ARPS) on when new democracies built welfare states — feeds the first-decade measurement design.

F. Acemoglu, Egorov & Sonin (2013, QJE) — the pre-commitment mechanism [15 min]
Locate: NBER w17357 PDF (free) or Acemoglu's MIT page. grep: "populist", "redistribut", "inequality", "Proposition", "equilibrium", "commit".
Record: (a) model skeleton: agents, candidates (established elite vs populist), timing, what the populist promises; (b) the "endogenous limits to redistribution" result — why high inequality makes the elite candidate choose low redistribution and how that opens the populist equilibrium; (c) plain-English mechanism statement usable in the paper; (d) any comparative statics that map to observable proxies (e.g., low pre-commitment redistribution + high inequality ⇒ polarization/populist support). This operationalizes mechanism (iii) of the gate.

G. Policy feedback + universalism micro-evidence [20 min, trim to 10 if behind]
Fetch abstracts/full texts as available: Pierson (1993) "When Effect Becomes Cause" (World Politics); Campbell (2012) "Policy Makes Mass Politics" (ARPS, open); Korpi & Palme (1998) "Paradox of Redistribution" (ASR: universalism reduces inequality more than targeting — the exact result that justifies measuring the gate with universal services, not CCTs); Skocpol (1991) "Targeting within Universalism"; Manacorda, Miguel & Vigorito (2011, AEJ:Applied) PANES→incumbent support; Zucco (2013) Bolsa Família→Lula vote; Dolls, Fuest & Peichl (2012, JPubE) automatic stabilizers absorb ≈38% EU vs≈32% US of income shocks (mechanism i quantification); CEQ/“Commitment to Equity” (Lustig et al.) benefit-incidence studies — health & education spending benefiting lower/middle deciles in developing countries (mechanism ii); IMF Fiscal Monitor (Oct 2017) middle-class fiscal redistribution; EBRD Life in Transition (public-service satisfaction in transition countries, if findable in 5 min).
Record per item: one-line takeaway + citation + which mechanism (i)/(ii)/(iii) or the Cash-vs-Services corollary it supports.

H. Supporting regime-data papers [10 min]
Lührmann-Tannenberg-Lindberg (2018, Democratization) — the RoW typology and transition-year conventions (what counts as entering "electoral democracy"; list gates). Boix-Miller-Rosato (2013, CPS) — democracy definition used by Rau-Stokes's sample screen; any later updates (BMR 2018/2023 extensions) — note vintage to use. V-Dem "Backsliding in the World" reports only if instantly accessible (they provide the 2020-2023 episode list context). Record: definitional thresholds + the transition window rule for coding "post-1990 democratizer" (1989-2005).

PHASE 2 — OWID AVAILABILITY AUDIT (2:15-2:30, 15 min)
Verify, by fetch of each OWID page (no downloads): that these series exist, their source and time coverage, and note the slugs for iter-2.
(a) liberal-democracy-index (V-Dem v2x_libdem); (b) electoral-democracy-index + Regimes of the World categories if present; (c) gini-coefficient-of-income-inequality (World Bank PIP) and the WID Gini page (income-inequality-gini-coefficient); (d) WID top-10% share page; (e) domestic health expenditure as share of GDP (WHO GGHE-D) — scrutinize start years (many countries pre-2000 missing); (f) government expenditure on education as share of GDP (WB EdStats); (g) total social expenditure (% GDP, OECD SOCX) — record the metadata note on composition (pensions share) for the placebo arm; (h) political polarization (V-Dem v2x_polarization) — does OWID host it?; (i) ILO education & mismatch series (educated/youth unemployment by education level) — does OWID host it? If (h)/(i) absent, that goes in follow_up_questions (iter-2 may need V-Dem/WID raw or an OWID proxy: overall youth unemployment, school enrollment).
Record in notes/08_owid_audit.md: slug, exact title, source attribution, date coverage, and any caveats (e.g., "WHO GGHE-D starts 2000" — relevant because first-decade end for 1989-95 transitions is 1999-2005, so pre-2000 values must be proxied by enrollment/spending alternatives or later-start values).

PHASE 3 — SYNTHESIS: THE DESIGN BRIEFING (2:30-2:50, 20 min)
Distill everything into the mechanism→specification mapping that iteration 2 will implement. The report MUST contain, for each of the five specifications, the concrete form: variables, lags, FE, clustering, sample, controls, and the source(s) each element is taken from:
1. GATE INTERACTION (the core): e.g., LDI_{c,t} ~ Gini_{c,t-5} + Welf_c0 + Gini × Welf_c0 + controls, country+year FE (or long differences), cluster country; Welf_c0 = health+edu spending %GDP at first-decade end, time-invariant. Expectations from theory: β(Gini)≈0 in high-Welf arm; strongly negative in thin arm.
2. ADJUDICATION SPEC: same sample, first WITHOUT interaction (baseline main effect — compare magnitude/sign to Rau-Stokes A), then WITH interaction — reconciles Rau-Stokes (main effect nonzero overall) vs Haggard-Kaufman (no systematic economic grievance) by showing the effect is concentrated in the thin-welfare arm. Record from dossier A the exact Rau-Stokes estimate for calibration.
3. EROSION-ONSET EVENT STUDIES + LEAD-LAG SEQUENCING: dynamic coefficients around erosion onset (−5..+5) estimated separately by welfare arm; sequencing tests (does inequality surge / public-service retrenchment precede LDI decline by 3-5y in thin arm only? no anticipatory welfare decline in high arm?). Ground onset definition in Rau-Stokes episodes (A) and Lührmann's sequencing framework (H); reverse-causality guard from Szikra-Öktem 2023 (welfare as outcome of backsliding) — search that paper quickly if time permits (10 min) or cite from abstract.
4. WELFARE-DEPENDENT DIVIDEND TEST: ANRR 2015 event-study design (B) restricted to post-1990 democratizers, split by first-decade welfare legacy; outcome: WID top-10% share (and health+edu spending); then correlate first-decade dividend size with 2010-2022 LDI resilience.
5. MECHANISM ARMS: (a) polarization: v2x_polarization as outcome of Gini × thin-welfare (mediation toward LDI decline) — source E's polarization emphasis; (b) mismatch: Campante-Chor interaction template (D) transposed: schooling/edu-expansion × ILO educated-youth-unemployment × thin welfare on ΔLDI.
Also produce the DIVERGENCES-TO-BRIDGE section (the gate design differs from prior work on these axes: Rau-Stokes uninterrupted-democracy sample 1995-2020 vs young democratizers 1989-2005 — the iter-2 sample must retain below-threshold democratizers with continuous LDI; Houle's coup-era survival vs graded erosion; ANRR's 1960-2010 all-democratizations dividend vs the post-1990 wave; SOCX pension-heavy total spending vs universal services measure (placebo arm); H&K's case-based evidence vs the interaction design) and a MEASUREMENT-RISK section (PIP Gini 5-yr waves & coverage in this sample; WHO GGHE-D start years; OWID vintage/anchor-year caveats) listing which items landed in follow_up_questions.

PHASE 4 — WRITE OUTPUTS (2:50-3:00, 10 min)
research_report.md sections: 1) Executive summary (the five specs + key numbers in 1 page); 2) Per-source extraction dossier (paper→specs, with quotes+URLs); 3) Mechanism→specification mapping table; 4) Divergences & bridging decisions; 5) OWID availability audit; 6) Measurement risks & unknown; 7) Full source list with URLs/DOIs.
research_out.json: answer = ≤800-word synthesis hitting: what each study contributes, the five specs, the two must-bridge divergences, the top 3 data risks; sources = every source actually used (title, url, doi, one-line contribution; mark PAYWALLED-ABSTRACT-ONLY where applicable); follow_up_questions = actionable questions for iteration 2 (see examples in §2 of this plan are not enough — list at minimum the OWID-hosting questions for polarization and ILO mismatch, the Rau-Stokes replication-data question, PIP coverage for the youth sample, health-spending pre-2000 proxy, SOCX composition confirmation).
Validate research_out.json with a JSON parser (python -m json.tool) before finishing.

PHASE 5 — QA (final 5 min of Phase 4 or as needed)
- No citation without a fetched source; no unexplained numbers; every number in the briefing has a URL+quote in notes.
- Check the five specifications are each grounded in ≥1 dossier.
- Any item you could not extract is explicitly listed as "UNVERIFIED" in the dossier and mirrored into follow_up_questions.

FAILURE SCENARIOS & FALLBACKS
- Paywall (QJE, World Politics, CUP, ASR): use NBER/author PDFs (Campante-Chor w16752, AES w17357, ANRR w19746, Houle's MSU page), Google Books preview for H&K, journal abstracts, and published reviews for case lists. Mark PAYWALLED-ABSTRACT-ONLY; extract what abstracts give; never fabricate.
- PNAS SI inaccessible: use main text; record which tables came from SI vs main.
- Search engine failure / rate limit: switch to --mode scholarly (OpenAlex/Crossref); retry with backoff; if the ability server is down, say so in the report instead of improvising.
- Time pressure: prioritize A (Rau-Stokes), B (ANRR), E (H&K), D (Campante-Chor), then OWID audit; G/H trim to abstracts; F compress.
- Contradictory info across sources: record both with URLs (e.g., ANRR venue in the NBER abstract vs ScienceDirect chapter page) and flag for the experiment (citations matter).

CONSTRAINTS
- Zero LLM spend (no OpenRouter calls needed for this artifact; the $10 budget is not consumed by web research). All work uses free web tooling.
- Everything written under this workspace (absolute path /ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_2nz_vV2E7aIl/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_plan/gen_plan_research_2/... or its run directory equivalents — the executor's own workspace).
- Python only for JSON validation; no data computation, no downloads of datasets, no model estimation — this artifact is pure information gathering + synthesis.

GATE INTERACTION (core): e.g., LDI_{c,t} ~ Gini_{c,t-5} + Welf_c0 + (Gini x Welf_c0) + controls, country+year FE, cluster by country; Welf_c0 = health+edu spending %GDP at first-decade end, time-invariant (pre-determined). Theory expectation: inequality effect ~0 in the high-welfare arm, strongly negative in the thin arm (use dossier B's dividend logic + dossier F's commitment logic as the theoretical underpinning; dossier G for the universalism justification).\\n2. ADJUDICATION SPEC: same sample, first WITHOUT interaction (baseline main effect \u2014 compare sign/magnitude to dossier A's Rau-Stokes estimate), then WITH interaction. This is the empirical reconciliation of Rau-Stokes (non-zero main effect) vs Haggard-Kaufman (no systematic economic grievance): the effect is concentrated in the thin-welfare arm.\\n3. EROSION-ONSET EVENT STUDIES + LEAD-LAG SEQUENCING: dynamic coefficients around erosion onset (-5..+5) estimated separately by welfare arm; sequencing tests (inequality surge and public-service retrenchment LEAD LDI decline by 3-5 years in the thin arm only; no anticipatory welfare decline before erosion in the high arm). MECHANISM ARMS: (a) polarization: v2x_polarization as outcome of Gini x thin-welfare (mediation toward LDI decline) \u2014 anchored in dossier E's emphasis on polarization as proximate cause; (b) mismatch: Campante-Chor interaction template (dossier D) transposed: schooling/education expansion x ILO educated-youth-unemployment x thin welfare on Delta-LDI, with OWID availability from Phase 2.\\nAlso produce: DIVERGENCES-TO-BRIDGE section \u2014 (i) Rau-Stokes uninterrupted-democracy sample 1995-2020 vs young democratizers 1989-2005: iter-2 must KEEP below-threshold democratizers with the continuous LDI outcome instead of censoring them; (ii) SOCX pension-heavy total spending vs the universal-services measure (health+edu) \u2014 the placebo arm; (iii) Houle's coup-era discrete survival vs graded erosion-era outcome; (iv) ANRR's 1960-2010 all-democratizations dividend vs the post-1990 wave; (v) H&K's case-based evidence vs the interaction design; and a MEASUREMENT-RISK section \u2014 PIP Gini 5-year waves/coverage in the young-democratizer sample, WHO GGHE-D pre-2000 gaps, WID interpolation, OWID vintage/anchor-year caveats \u2014 listing which items must go to follow_up_questions.\\n\\nPHASE 4 \u2014 WRITE OUTPUTS (2:50-3:00, 10 min)\\nresearch_report.md sections: 1) Executive summary (the five specs + key calibration numbers, <=1 page); The only scripts used are the web-tool search/fetch/grep helpers.\",\n  \"explanation\": \"Iteration 2's experiment can only adjudicate Rau-Stokes versus Haggard-Kaufman through the welfare gate if it faithfully reuses their exact measurement and design choices instead of reinventing them: the Rau-Stokes sample screen, erosion-episode coding, Gini source and lag, controls, model family and clustering determine whether the iteration-2 baseline main effect is comparable to the flagship finding; the ANRR event-study form, outcome list (top-decile share, public goods), and timing conventions size the welfare-dependent dividend test; Houle's survival setup justifies the first-democratic-decade framing; Campante-Chor's schooling-times-opportunities interaction is the template for the educated-youth-mismatch arm; Haggard-Kaufman's backslider/survivor case list enables the qualitative-quantitative bridge and a sensitivity analysis; Acemoglu-Egorov-Sonin's endogenous-limits-to-redistribution logic operationalizes the pre-commitment mechanism; and the policy-feedback/universalism evidence (Pierson, Campbell, Korpi-Palme, Manacorda et al., Dolls-Fuest-Peichl, CEQ incidence) supplies the micro-foundations for the constituency-formation and automatic-stabilization mechanisms as well as the cash-vs-universal-services corollary. Public Economics) automatic stabilizers absorb ~38% (EU) vs ~32% (US) of income shocks (quantifies mechanism i); CEQ / \\\"Commitment to Equity\\\" (Lustig et al.) benefit-incidence evidence that health+education spending reaches lower/middle deciles in developing countries (mechanism ii); IMF Fiscal Monitor (Oct 2017) on fiscal redistribution and the middle class. Record per item: one-line takeaway + citation + which gate mechanism (i)/(ii)/(iii) or the Cash-vs-Services corollary it supports.\\n\\nH. Supporting regime-data papers [10 min]\\nLuehrmann-Tannenberg-Lindberg (2018, Democratization): RoW typology, what qualifies as entering \\\"electoral democracy\\\", transition-year conventions. Boix-Miller-Rosato (2013, CPS): democracy definition used as sample screen; note vintage/updates. Record: definitional thresholds + the rule for coding \\\"post-1990 democratizer\\\" (transition 1989-2005) and \\\"uninterrupted democracy\\\".\\n\\nPHASE 2 \u2014 OWID AVAILABILITY AUDIT (2:15-2:30, 15 min)\\nVerify by fetching each OWID page (pages only, NO dataset downloads): series exists, its source attribution, date coverage; record slug + caveats in notes/08_owid_audit.md:\\n(a) liberal-democracy-index (V-Dem v2x_libdem); (b) electoral-democracy-index and Regimes-of-the-World categories if hosted; (c) World Bank PIP Gini page and the WID Gini page; (d) WID top-10% share page; (e) domestic-health-expenditure-share-gdp (WHO GGHE-D) \u2014 CRITICAL: scrutinize start years (many countries have no pre-2000 values; first-decade end for 1989-95 transitions is 1999-2005); (f) government-expenditure-on-education-share-of-gdp (WB EdStats); (g) total-social-expenditure-gdp-oecd (OECD SOCX) \u2014 record any metadata on composition (pensions share) for the placebo arm; (h) political-polarization page (V-Dem v2x_polarization) \u2014 hosted?; (i) ILO education/mismatch series (unemployment by education level, especially educated youth) \u2014 hosted? If (h)/(i) are absent, that goes into follow_up_questions with OWID proxy candidates (overall youth unemployment, school enrollment).\\n\\nPHASE 3 \u2014 SYNTHESIS: THE DESIGN BRIEFING (2:30-2:50, 20 min)\\nDistill everything into the mechanism->specification mapping iteration 2 will implement. The report MUST specify, for each of the five specifications, the concrete form \u2014 variables, lags, fixed effects, clustering, sample, controls \u2014 and cite the source(s) each element comes from:\\n1. GATE INTERACTION (core): e.g., LDI_{c,t} ~ Gini_{c,t-5} + Welf_c0 + (Gini x Welf_c0) + controls, country+year FE, cluster by country; Welf_c0 = health+edu spending %GDP at first-decade end, time-invariant (pre-determined). Theory expectation: inequality effect ~0 in the high-welfare arm, strongly negative in the thin arm (use dossier B's dividend logic + dossier F's commitment logic as the theoretical underpinning; dossier G for the universalism justification).\\n2. ADJUDICATION SPEC: same sample, first WITHOUT interaction (baseline main effect \u2014 compare sign/magnitude to dossier A's Rau-Stokes estimate), then WITH interaction. This is the empirical reconciliation of Rau-Stokes (non-zero main effect) vs Haggard-Kaufman (no systematic economic grievance): the effect is concentrated in the thin-welfare arm.\\n3. EROSION-ONSET EVENT STUDIES + LEAD-LAG SEQUENCING: dynamic coefficients around erosion onset (-5..+5) estimated separately by welfare arm; sequencing tests (inequality surge and public-service retrenchment LEAD LDI decline by 3-5 years in the thin arm only; no anticipatory welfare decline before erosion in the high arm). Ground onset in dossier A's episode list and dossier H's sequencing framework; guard reverse causality using the Szikra-Oektem (2023, JESP) finding that welfare states are transformed UNDER backsliding \u2014 quick search for that paper (<=10 min) or cite from its abstract.\\n4. WELFARE-DEPENDENT DIVIDEND TEST: dossier B's ANRR event-study design restricted to post-1990 democratizers, split by first-decade welfare legacy; outcome: WID top-10% share (plus health+edu spending); then test whether first-decade dividend size predicts 2010-2022 LDI resilience.\\n5. MECHANISM ARMS: (a) polarization: v2x_polarization as outcome of Gini x thin-welfare (mediation toward LDI decline) \u2014 anchored in dossier E's emphasis on polarization as proximate cause; (b) mismatch: Campante-Chor interaction template (dossier D) transposed: schooling/education expansion x ILO educated-youth-unemployment x thin welfare on Delta-LDI, with OWID availability from Phase 2.\\nAlso produce: DIVERGENCES-TO-BRIDGE section \u2014 (i) Rau-Stokes uninterrupted-democracy sample 1995-2020 vs young democratizers 1989-2005: iter-2 must KEEP below-threshold democratizers with the continuous LDI outcome instead of censoring them; (ii) SOCX pension-heavy total spending vs the universal-services measure (health+edu) \u2014 the placebo arm; (iii) Houle's coup-era discrete survival vs graded erosion-era outcome; (iv) ANRR's 1960-2010 all-democratizations dividend vs the post-1990 wave; (v) H&K's case-based evidence vs the interaction design; and a MEASUREMENT-RISK section \u2014 PIP Gini 5-year waves/coverage in the young-democratizer sample, WHO GGHE-D pre-2000 gaps, WID interpolation, OWID vintage/anchor-year caveats \u2014 listing which items must go to follow_up_questions.\\n\\nPHASE 4 \u2014 WRITE OUTPUTS (2:50-3:00, 10 min)\\nresearch_report.md sections: 1) Executive summary (the five specs + key calibration numbers, <=1 page); 2) Per-source extraction dossier (paper -> specs with quotes + URLs); 3) Mechanism->specification mapping table; 4) Divergences & bridging decisions; 5) OWID availability audit; 6) Measurement risks and open items; 7) Full source list with URLs/DOIs (mark PAYWALLED-ABSTRACT-ONLY where applicable).\\nresearch_out.json: answer = <=800-word synthesis covering: what each study contributes, the five specs at specification level, the two must-bridge divergences, and the top-3 data risks; sources = every source actually used (title, url, doi, one-line contribution); follow_up_questions = actionable questions for iteration 2, including at minimum: (a) does OWID host V-Dem polarization and ILO educated-youth unemployment (and which proxy if not)?; (b) is Rau-Stokes replication data (episode list) public?; (c) PIP Gini coverage for post-1990 democratizers vs WID top-10 as treatment?; (d) WHO GGHE-D pre-2000 gaps \u2014 proxy with enrollment/immunization or later-start values?; (e) OECD SOCX composition confirmation for the placebo; (f) which regime classification (RoW vs BMR) defines the transition window?\\nValidate research_out.json with a JSON parser if available (python -m json.tool via the tooling venv); otherwise manual review.\\n\\nPHASE 5 \u2014 QA (final minutes, fold into Phase 4)\\n- No citation without a fetched source; no unexplained number; every number in the briefing has a URL+quote in notes.\\n- Each of the five specifications is grounded in >=1 dossier.\\n- Any item not extracted is explicitly marked UNVERIFIED in its dossier and mirrored into follow_up_questions.\\n- Outputs exist, JSON is valid, and the report is self-contained enough for iteration 2's experiment designer to implement specs 1-5 without re-reading the primary papers.\\n\\nFAILURE SCENARIOS & FALLBACKS\\n- Paywall (QJE, World Politics, ASR, CUP): NBER/author PDFs (Campante-Chor w16752, AES w17357, ANRR w19746, Houle's MSU page), Google Books preview for H&K, journal abstracts, and published reviews for case lists. Mark PAYWALLED-ABSTRACT-ONLY; extract what is available; never fabricate.\\n- PNAS SI inaccessible: work from the main text; note which tables came from the SI.\\n- Search engine failure / rate limits: switch to --mode scholarly (OpenAlex/Crossref); retry with backoff; if the ability server is down, report it rather than improvising.\\n- Time pressure: prioritize A (Rau-Stokes), B (ANRR dividend), E (H&K case list), D (Campante-Chor), then the OWID audit; trim G/H to abstracts; compress F to the mechanism statement.\\n- Conflicting metadata (e.g., ANRR venue): record both with URLs and flag for the experiment (the citation matters for the paper's references).\\n\\nCONSTRAINTS\\n- Zero LLM spend: this artifact needs NO OpenRouter calls (the $10 budget is untouched); all work uses free web tooling.\\n- All files written inside the executor's workspace (absolute paths, subdirectories notes/ OK).\\n- No dataset downloads, no data computation, no model estimation \u2014 this is pure information gathering + synthesis. The only scripts used are the web-tool search/fetch/grep helpers.\",\n  \"explanation\": \"Iteration 2's experiment can only adjudicate Rau-Stokes versus Haggard-Kaufman through the welfare gate if it faithfully reuses their exact measurement and design choices instead of reinventing them: the Rau-Stokes sample screen, erosion-episode coding, Gini source and lag, controls, model family and clustering determine whether the iteration-2 baseline main effect is comparable to the flagship finding; the ANRR event-study form, outcome list (top-decile share, public goods), and timing conventions size the welfare-dependent dividend test; Houle's survival setup justifies the first-democratic-decade framing; Campante-Chor's schooling-times-opportunities interaction is the template for the educated-youth-mismatch arm; Haggard-Kaufman's backslider/survivor case list enables the qualitative-quantitative bridge and a sensitivity analysis; Acemoglu-Egorov-Sonin's endogenous-limits-to-redistribution logic operationalizes the pre-commitment mechanism; and the policy-feedback/universalism evidence (Pierson, Campbell, Korpi-Palme, Manacorda et al., Dolls-Fuest-Peichl, CEQ incidence) supplies the micro-foundations for the constituency-formation and automatic-stabilization mechanisms as well as the cash-vs-universal-services corollary. The OWID availability audit additionally de-risks the hard constraint that everything be testable on OWID panels (LDI, PIP/WID inequality, GGHE-D and EdStats spending, SOCX placebo, polarization, ILO mismatch) before any experiment is written. The deliverable is a design briefing that maps every hypothesis mechanism to one of five concrete specifications \u2014 gate interaction, adjudication (main effect with and without interaction), erosion-onset event studies with lead-lag sequencing, the welfare-dependent dividend test, and the polarization/mismatch mechanism arms \u2014 and flags the divergences that must be bridged, most importantly the Rau-Stokes uninterrupted-democracy sample versus the young-democratizer sample and the pension-heavy SOCX composition versus the universal-services measure. Without this briefing, the experiment would risk either replicating a main effect that everyone already knows or testing a gate whose operationalizations no longer connect to the literature it claims to reconcile.\",\n  \"doi\": null,\n  \"url\": null\n}", "kind": "FileEditorObservation"}
